Concepts
Authentication
Copy page Copy page
Understand principals, tokens, and how to authenticate with the Devin API
Copy page Copy page
The Devin API uses a principal + token model. A principal is who you are (your identity), and a token is how you prove it (your credential). Understanding this distinction is the key to choosing the right authentication method for your use case.
Principals & tokens
Principal Token Description
Service User (non-human) Service User API Key For automated integrations and CI/CD pipelines
User (human) Personal Access Token (PAT) For human programmatic access under your own identity
All API credentials use the cog_ prefix format. Include your token in the Authorization header of every request:
curl -X GET "https://api.devin.ai/v3/organizations/ $DEVIN_ORG_ID /sessions" \
-H "Authorization: Bearer cog_your_token_here"
The key distinction is which principal the token authenticates as :
A Service User API Key authenticates as the service user — the service user’s identity, permissions, and org memberships apply.
A Personal Access Token authenticates as the human user who created it — that user’s identity, permissions, and org memberships apply.
Personal access token or service user?
Personal access token Service user API key
Acts as You A separate non-human identity
Permissions Exactly yours, in every organization you have access to The role you give the service user, in the organizations it is added to
Scope Your account. Each request names the organization Created at the organization or enterprise level
Audit trail Attributed to you Attributed to the service user, even when it creates a session on someone’s behalf with create_as_user_id
Lifecycle Tied to you. Revoked automatically if you leave the account Not tied to any person, so it survives team changes
Best for Personal scripts, local tooling, the Devin CLI and desktop apps CI/CD, production integrations, shared automation
Rule of thumb: if the integration should keep working after you leave the team or change roles, use a service user. If it should do exactly what you can do, as you, use a PAT.
Service users (recommended for automation)
Service users are non-human accounts designed for API integrations. They can be assigned specific permissions via RBAC and added to organizations independently of human users.
How it works
Provision a service user in Settings > Devin API ( Service users tab) for an organization, or Enterprise settings > Devin API ( Service users tab) for the enterprise
Assign a role that controls which endpoints the service user can access. You pick a role from a dropdown, not individual permissions
Choose an expiration — it defaults to 30 days; the other options are 7 days, 90 days, and, where your account allows it, Never
Copy the API key — the key starts with cog_ and is shown only once, right after provisioning
Use the key in the Authorization header of every API request
curl -X POST "https://api.devin.ai/v3/organizations/ $DEVIN_ORG_ID /sessions" \
-H "Authorization: Bearer $DEVIN_API_KEY " \
-H "Content-Type: application/json" \
-d '{"prompt": "Create a simple Python script"}'
Service user scopes
Scope Created in API access Use case
Organization Settings > Devin API > Service users /v3/organizations/* Session management, knowledge, playbooks, secrets
Enterprise Enterprise settings > Devin API > Service users /v3/enterprise/* + /v3/organizations/* Cross-org management, analytics, audit logs, billing
Find your organization ID at the top of the Settings > Devin API page.
Session attribution with create_as_user_id
Service users are separate identities from human users. By default, sessions created by a service user are attributed to the service user itself. To create sessions on behalf of a specific user , pass the create_as_user_id parameter when creating a session. The session will appear in that user’s session list and count toward their usage.
This requires the ImpersonateOrgSessions permission on the service user’s role.
Key properties
Keys start with cog_ and are shown only once at creation time
Service users appear separately from human users in audit logs
Permissions are controlled via RBAC — assign only what the integration needs
Enterprise service users inherit org-level permissions across all organizations
For detailed setup instructions, see the Teams quick start or Enterprise quick start .
Personal access tokens
Personal Access Tokens (PATs) allow human users to authenticate programmatically under their own identity. When you use a PAT, the API sees you — your permissions, your org memberships, and your audit trail.
This is useful when you want to make API calls as yourself (e.g., personal scripts, local tooling) rather than through a shared service user.
A PAT is not scoped to an organization or an enterprise. It belongs to you within your account and works in every organization you have access to. Each request names an organization in the URL, and your role in that organization decides what the request can do.
For enterprise accounts, PATs are governed by an enterprise-wide policy (disabled by default) that enterprise admins configure — including an approval workflow and mandatory token expiration.
For more details, see Personal Access Tokens .
Legacy authentication (deprecated)
Legacy API keys are deprecated. Use API v3 with service user authentication. See the migration guide .
Legacy API keys are used with the v1 and v2 APIs. They continue to work during the deprecation period but do not support new features like RBAC, session attribution, or cursor-based pagination.
Key type Prefix Used with Description
Personal API key apk_user_ v1, v2 Tied to a user account, inherits user permissions
Service API key apk_ v1 Organization-scoped, for automation
Where to generate: Settings > API Keys
Security best practices
Never share API keys in publicly accessible areas such as GitHub repositories, client-side code, or logs.
Store keys securely : Use environment variables or secret management systems
Rotate keys regularly : Generate new keys and revoke old ones periodically
Use service users for automation : Prefer service users over personal keys for production
Apply least privilege : Grant only the minimum permissions required
Monitor usage : Review audit logs for unexpected API activity
Revoke compromised keys immediately : If a key is exposed, revoke it and generate a new one. In Settings > Devin API , a service user’s key has no revoke or rotate button: use Delete service user on its row, which stops the key immediately, then provision a new one. Enterprises can also revoke or rotate a service user’s key through the API (beta)
Troubleshooting
401 Unauthorized
Possible causes:
Invalid or expired API key
Missing Authorization header
Incorrect Bearer token format
Using a legacy API key ( apk_ / apk_user_ ) with the Devin MCP — only cog_ -prefixed keys are supported
Solution: Verify your API key is correct and properly formatted in the Authorization header. For MCP usage, ensure you are using a service user API key (not a legacy key).
403 Forbidden
Possible causes:
API key doesn’t have required permissions
Using the wrong key type for the endpoint (e.g., legacy key with v3 endpoints)
Attempting to access resources outside your scope
Solution:
Ensure your service user has the correct role and permissions
For legacy v2 endpoints: ensure you have the Enterprise Admin role
For legacy v1 endpoints: verify you have access to the organization
404 Not Found
Possible causes:
Incorrect API endpoint URL
Resource doesn’t exist or you don’t have access
Solution: Verify the endpoint URL and that the resource exists.

Each request names an organization in the URL, and your role in that organization decides what the request can do.`}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.p,{children:`For enterprise accounts, PATs are governed by an enterprise-wide policy (disabled by default) that enterprise admins configure — including an approval workflow and mandatory token expiration.`}),`\n`,_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`For more details, see `,_jsx(_components.a,{href:`/api-reference/personal-access-tokens`,children:`Personal Access Tokens`}),`.`]}),`\n`,_jsx(_components.hr,{}),`\n`,_jsx(Heading,{level:`2`,id:`legacy-authentication-deprecated`,children:`Legacy authentication (deprecated)`}),`\n`,_jsx(Warning,{children:_jsxs(_components.p,{children:[`Legacy API keys are deprecated.
